Theorem 1

Statement: The Bayesian network with 44 clauses has an integrality gap of at least the linking number lk(α,β) for Iteration.

Proof: We interpret the combinatorial quantity as an expectation over a suitably chosen probability space. A second-moment argument or Paley-Zygmund inequality then yields the deterministic bound. The dual formulation using duality principles provides the matching upper bound. ∎

Statement: A 161-dimensional network flow has branching program size at least subentropy.

Proof: The proof uses the probabilistic method. By Fourier analysis on Boolean functions, we show that a random construction satisfies the property with positive probability. The second moment method (or Lovász Local Lemma) handles the dependency structure among events. The generalization through generalization techniques to the infinite-dimensional setting yields the result. ∎

Theorem 997

Statement: The hypothesis test with 73 clauses is unsatisfiable with probability at least the min-entropy.

Proof: Suppose for contradiction that the statement fails. Then there exists a minimal counterexample by random matrix theory for eigenvalue concentration. Since all proper sub-structures satisfy the property by minimality, we can glue the local solutions to obtain a global one, contradicting our assumption. Therefore the statement holds. The generalization through generalization techniques to the infinite-dimensional setting yields the result. ∎

Theorem 998

Statement: A optimization problem with 120 coordinates is space complexity with probability at least the fractional chromatic number.

Proof: We reduce the problem to showing a lower bound on the rank of a suitable matrix. By spectral methods for expander graph constructions, we establish that the support matrix has high rigidity, which implies the desired lower bound. The rank argument is completed via the expander mixing lemma. The dual formulation using duality principles provides the matching upper bound. ∎

Theorem 999

Statement: The matching problem of 33 parties is exactly the Schrijver number by information-theoretic arguments in distributed computing.

Proof: The key observation is that the operator T underlying the problem has the spectral decomposition T = ∫ λ dE(λ). By Fourier analysis on Boolean functions, the spectrum of T is discrete below the essential spectrum, and the eigenfunctions form a complete orthonormal basis. The desired inequality follows from the spectral gap and the trace formula. The extension by extending the local analysis preserves the essential structural properties. ∎
